A Non-asymptotic Analysis of Generalized Approximate Message Passing Algorithms with Right Rotationally Invariant Designs
Collin Cademartori, Cynthia Rush

Abstract
Approximate Message Passing (AMP) algorithms are a class of iterative procedures for computationally-efficient estimation in high-dimensional inference and estimation tasks. Due to the presence of an ‘Onsager’ correction term in its iterates, for N × M N\times M design matrices 𝐀 \mathbf{A} with i.i.d. Gaussian entries, the asymptotic distribution of the estimate at any iteration of the algorithm can be exactly characterized in the large system limit as M / N → δ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) M/N\to\delta\in(0,\infty) via a scalar recursion referred to as state evolution. In this paper, we show that appropriate functionals of the iterates, in fact, concentrate around their limiting values predicted by these asymptotic distributions with rates exponentially fast in N N for a large class of AMP-style algorithms, including those that are used when high-dimensional generalized linear regression models are assumed to be the data-generating process, like the generalized AMP algorithm, or those that are used when the measurement matrix is assumed to be right rotationally invariant instead of i.i.d. Gaussian, like vector AMP and generalized vector AMP. In practice, these more general AMP algorithms have many applications, for example in in communications or imaging, and this work provides the first study of finite sample behavior of such algorithms.



1
Introduction

Approximate message passing (AMP) algorithms are a class of iterative methods for solving various high-dimensional statistical estimation and inference problems
[
14
,
33
,
5
,
24
,
35
]
. In this paper we focus specifically on the problem of high-dimensional (generalized) linear regression. In particular, we consider estimating an unknown coefficient vector or signal
\mathbf{x}_{0}
, assuming knowledge of both the output
\mathbf{y}
and matrix
\mathbf{A}
, using models of the form

\begin{split}\mathbf{y}&\sim p\left(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\right),\qquad\mathbf{x}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}p(\mathbf{x}_{0}),\end{split}

(1.1)

where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
with
M<N
,
p(\mathbf{x}_{0})
is a prior distribution on the signal, and
p\left(\mathbf{y}\mid\cdot\right)
is some output distribution. We will give special consideration in the paper to the case where
p(\mathbf{y}\mid\cdot)
is a Gaussian distribution. Letting
f(\cdot)
denote a normal density, this allows us to model the problem as

\begin{split}\mathbf{y}&=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}+\mathbf{w},\qquad\mathbf{w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}f(\mathbf{w}),\qquad\mathbf{x}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}p(\mathbf{x}_{0}).\end{split}

(1.2)

AMP-style algorithms can accommodate a range of estimation procedures for the models in (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), including maximum a posteriori (MAP) and minimum mean squared error (MMSE) estimation. See
[
17
]
for a tutorial on AMP.

In its original form, AMP
is the following two-step iteration for recovering
\mathbf{x}_{0}
from knowledge of
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{A}
under the linear model of (
1.2
) and the assumption that
\mathbf{A}
is i.i.d. Gaussian. At iteration
k\geq 0
, the algorithm updates its estimate of the signal
\mathbf{x}_{0}
with estimates
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2},...
. Initializing with
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{-1}=\mathbf{v}_{-1}=\mathbf{1}
, calculate for
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}_{k}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k-1}+\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{v}_{k-1},\qquad\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k}=g_{k}(\mathbf{r}_{k}),

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k}+\frac{N}{M}\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\mathrm{div}[g_{k}(\mathbf{r}_{k})],

(1.4)

where
g_{k}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which acts elementwise on its vector input, is the so-called ‘denoiser’ function, an appropriately-chosen Lipschitz function depending on the estimation procedure to be performed, and
\mathrm{div}[g_{k}(\mathbf{r})]=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial[\mathbf{r}]_{i}}g_{k}([\mathbf{r}]_{i})
is the divergence of the denoiser, measuring the sensitivity of
g_{k}
at its input.
The
\mathbf{v}_{k}
update in (
1.4
) can be interpreted as a corrected residual: the usual residual
\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{A}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k}
with a so-called ‘Onsager correction’ given by
\frac{N}{M}\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\mathrm{div}[g_{k}(\mathbf{r}_{k})]
.

With the presence of the correction term in the residual step,
it is possible under certain conditions to characterize the asymptotic distribution of
\mathbf{r}_{k}
in the large system limit where
N\to\infty
and
{M}/{N}\to\delta\in(0,1)
(see
[
5
,
8
]
). In particular, for variances
\tau_{k}
that can be characterized exactly by a scalar recursion referred to as state evolution, in this limit, the elements of the vectors
\mathbf{r}_{k}
behave like samples from a Gaussian distribution with mean vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and covariance matrix
\tau_{k}\mathbf{I}_{N}
, denoted henceforth as
N(\mathbf{x}_{0},\tau_{k}\mathbf{I}_{N})
, where
\mathbf{x}_{0}
is the true signal and
\mathbf{I}_{N}
is an
N\times N
identity matrix.

More formally, it is shown in
[
5
]
that for all pseudo-Lipschitz (defined in Section
3
) loss functions
\phi
, in the large system limit, empirical averages converge to deterministic limits,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi([\mathbf{r}_{k}]_{i},[\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i})\rightarrow\mathbb{E}\{\phi(X_{0}+\tau_{k}Z,X_{0})\},

(1.5)

where
Z\sim N(0,1)
is independent of
X_{0}\sim p(\mathbf{x}_{0})
. In
[
38
]
, this distributional convergence is refined with a finite sample analysis showing that these empirical averages
exhibit concentration around their limits with rates exponential in
N
up to
t=O\left(\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}\right)
iterations.

However, asymptotic guarantees for the AMP algorithm in (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) have been primarily studied in settings where the elements of the design matrices
\mathbf{A}
are i.i.d. sub-Gaussian
[
5
,
3
]
(though, an active line of recent research studies
universality
properties of AMP algorithms more generally; see Section
1.1
) and the concentration results only when the elements are i.i.d. Gaussian. Furthermore, considering the linear models in (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) concentration results have only been given for AMP for the model in (
1.2
), which is limited to the case of Gaussian outputs. In particular, there has been no concentration results developed for generalized versions of AMP, which can handle models such as (
1.1
), or for AMP algorithms with measurement matrices that are not i.i.d. Gaussian, despite such algorithms playing an important role in high-dimensional statistics
[
10
,
41
,
12
]
, wireless communications
[
22
,
7
]
, and many other applications
[
42
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
1
,
44
]
.

In this work, we will extend the finite sample analysis for AMP for the linear model in (
1.2
) given in
[
38
]
for a suite of AMP-style algorithms that can be used when it is assumed that
(i)
the data follows the generalized linear model in (
1.1
),
(ii)
the random design matrices
\mathbf{A}
comes from a class of right-rotationally invariant matrices (defined below), and
(iii)
both are assumed. In particular, generalized approximate message passing (GAMP)
[
35
]
, is an AMP-style algorithm used to perform estimation when
\mathbf{A}
is assumed to be i.i.d. Gaussian but the data come from the generalized linear regression model in (
1.1
). Moreover, vector approximate message passing (VAMP) and its generalized extension GVAMP are AMP-style algorithms, recently introduced in
[
36
]
and
[
40
]
, designed to perform estimation for the models (
1.2
) and (
1.1
), respectively, but under a much larger class of random design matrices
\mathbf{A}
.
In particular, this is the class of
right orthogonally invariant

\mathbf{A}
, meaning that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{V}
has the same distribution as
\mathbf{A}
for any
N\times N
orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{V}
. As we will see, this assumption represents a significant relaxation of the i.i.d. sub-Gaussian condition.

This work refines the existing GAMP and VAMP
[
36
,
35
,
21
]
asymptotics by providing a finite sample analysis of GVAMP – which works under the assumption of right rotationally invariant design matrices and generalized liner models (
1.1
) – and by extension to GAMP and VAMP as well. In particular, the non-asymptotic result for VAMP shown in this paper is given under only slightly stronger conditions than are used in the proof of its large system limit asymptotics in
[
36
]
. In more detail, in
[
36
]
, it was shown that empirical averages of pseudo-Lipschitz functions of the VAMP estimates
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi\left([\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}]_{i},[\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}\right)
converge in the large system limit (i.e. as
N\to\infty
) to their expected values under limiting distributions that can be characterized exactly. Here, we use concentration tools to show that this convergence in fact occurs exponentially fast in
N
and demonstrate how these results can be extended to the GVAMP and GAMP algorithms as well.

To summarize, this work generalizes the previous finite sample analyses for the AMP algorithm simultaneously in two main ways:

•

Our work covers GAMP and GVAMP algorithms for generalized linear problems in the form of
1.1
, with output distributions constrained only by a certain log-concavity requirement. This encompasses many common data models not covered by the Gaussian case, including, for example, logistic and Poisson regression.

•

Our analysis applies to a much less restrictive distributional assumption over the measurement matrix
\mathbf{A}
than previous finite sample analyses. In particular, our measurement matrix model samples the singular vector matrices uniformly from their orthogonal groups and allows the singular values to be marginally drawn from any arbitrary distribution with bounded support. This setup is substantially more general than the case of i.i.d. Gaussian matrices, and can in particular accommodate arbitrarily poor conditioning of the measurement matrix.

We emphasize that while refining earlier asymptotic results to provide non-asymptotic guarantees is useful to clarify the effect of the iteration number on the problem dimension and the sped of convergence to the state evolution predictions, it also has other application-specific implications; e.g. the finite sample analyses of
[
38
]
were used to establish the error exponent for sparse regression codes with AMP decoding for the additive white Gaussian noise channel
[
39
]
and the results in this work will be similarly useful in establishing error exponent for sparse regression codes with GAMP or VAMP decoding for more general channel models considered in
[
22
,
7
]
.

Structurally, the proof we provide is based on an approach used for the finite sample analysis of AMP in
[
38
]
; though, there are critical differences in the required analysis for GVAMP. In short, we develop a suite of concentration of measure tools to prove that, as the algorithm runs, the output retains concentration around its expected values. The idea is that if the algorithm concentrates through iteration
k
, then we can prove that it will concentrate at iteration
k+1
, with only a slightly degraded rate for the concentration. The differences between our proof and that given in
[
38
]
are detailed in Section
4.7
.
At a high level, when studying generalized versions of AMP algorithms, one must employ the output vector
\mathbf{y}
in the denoising functions, which is not the case in standard AMP. This causes added dependencies within the algorithm, as now the measurement matrix
\mathbf{A}
is no longer independent of the denoioser functions. On the other hand, studying the algorithm under rotationally invariant assumptions on
\mathbf{A}
(as opposed to i.i.d. Gaussian assumptions), also complicates the analyses by adding additional dependencies that need to be handled carefully in the concentration arguments (see point
(1)
in Section
4.7
).

1.1
Related Work

Recent work by Li and Wei
[
25
]
extends the finite sample analysis of
[
38
]
to a related AMP algorithm for spiked matrix estimation. This work shows rates exponential in
N
for up to
O\left(\frac{N}{\texttt{poly}\log N}\right)
iterations by using novel proof methods, improving on the
O\left(\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}\right)
iteration guarantees found in this paper and in
[
38
]
. An intriguing open question is whether their proof technique can be extended to AMP for linear and generalized linear regression, as is studied here.

As we mentioned above, AMP algorithms have been demonstrated both empirically and theoretically to perform well over a wide range of problems and to converge faster than the simple iterative soft thresholding method. However, there are now many variants on the original AMP algorithm. Remaining within the realm of Gaussian design matrices, for example, works have extended the AMP analyses to non-separable denoising functions that do not act componentwise on their vector arguments and can therefore take advantage of correlation between entries of the unknown signal
[
6
,
28
]
, to matrices with independent entries and a blockwise variance structure
[
23
,
13
]
, or to incorporate additional side information on the signal in order to accommodate streaming estimation procedures
[
26
]
. As mentioned, GAMP is designed to accommodate general separable output distributions; similarly for GVAMP. In other words, GAMP extends AMP to solve problems like (
1.1
). MLAMP
[
29
]
takes this idea further, accommodating multi-layer problems where separable nonlinear channels are stacked between multiple linear transformations.

In addition to VAMP, there have been other approaches to extending AMP to work under more general assumptions on the measurement matrix beyond i.i.d. Gaussian entries. These include the orthogonal approximate message passing algorithm (OAMP)
[
27
]
, extensions to matrices with i.i.d. sub-Gaussian entries
[
4
,
11
]
, semi-random matrices
[
15
]
, and other generalizations of AMP for rotationally invariant matrices
[
16
,
43
,
34
]
.
The OAMP algorithm is designed, like VAMP, to accommodate a larger class of design matrices
\mathbf{A}
. In particular, OAMP assumes that
\mathbf{A}
has distribution which is orthogonally invariant. If
\mathbf{A}
has singular value decomposition
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{T}
, then this requires that
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
are uniformly distributed and
\mathbf{U}
,
\mathbf{V}
, and
\mathbf{S}
are mutually independent. Extending finite sample analyses to more general classes of AMP algorithms like those discussed above is an exciting avenue for future work.

The types of generalized versions of AMP we study here, namely GAMP and GVAMP, which are designed for data models such as (
1.1
), and VAMP, which allows to extend beyond i.i.d. Gaussian measurement matrices, have found many applications in the literature to date. These algorithms play an important role in high-dimensional statistics
[
10
,
41
,
12
]
, wireless communications
[
22
,
7
]
, and many other applications
[
42
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
1
,
44
]
. In particular, the finite sample analysis for VAMP and GAMP presented here can be used to find the error rates for the capacity-achieving sparse regression coding schemes introduced in
[
22
,
7
]
, which use VAMP and GAMP as decoders.
Moreover, AMP algorithms are often used to establish computational limits of various high-dimensional statistics problems, as they are conjectured to be optimal amongst polynomial-time algorithms in some problem settings. In particular, finite sample analyses like that presented in this work, allow one to study such computational limits in the context of changing sparsity, where the fraction of nonzeros amongst the signal elements approaches zero as the sample size grows (as opposed to the fraction of nonzeros
being fixed in the limit). For example, in
a very simple Bernoulli-Rademacher signal model of rank-one matrix estimation from noisy observations, the work in
[
2
]
considers both information theoretic and computational limits, with the computational limits found via a finite sample analysis of the associated AMP algorithm. We believe the analyses in this work could be used to study computational limits for changing sparsity regimes in more general problem settings.

As we shall see below, the i.i.d. (sub-)Gaussian assumption is stronger than is needed to characterize the asymptotic behavior of (G)VAMP. And if one restricts attention to VAMP, the orthogonally invariant condition can be relaxed to the less stringent right orthogonally invariance condition. Furthermore, (G)VAMP has been demonstrated empirically to converge faster than GAMP across a range of design matrices (see
[
36
, Section VI]
).

We finally mention that part of this work was presented at ISIT 2020
[
9
]
. In particular,
[
9
]
provides the statement of the result for VAMP but does not include proof details, nor does it include the extension of these finite sample analyses to generalized versions of VAMP or AMP, namely to GVAMP or GAMP. These extensions are significant contributions beyond the original VAMP result, as they provide finite sample analyses for AMP algorithms for the generalized regression model of (
1.1
), whereas the VAMP result applied only to (
1.2
). Applications of the generalized versions of VAMP and AMP were discussed above.

1.2
Notation

Throughout, we use lowercase boldface letters like
\mathbf{v}
to denote vectors and uppercase boldface letters like
\mathbf{V}
to denote matrices. We use
\mathbf{V}^{T}
to denote the transpose of
\mathbf{V}
. For matrices
\mathbf{V}
, we use
\mathbf{V}_{ij}
to denote the element in the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
row and
j^{\mathrm{th}}
column. Similarly,
\mathbf{V}_{\cdot j}
is the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
columns of the matrix and
\mathbf{V}_{i\cdot}
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
row. For vectors
\mathbf{v}
, we use
[\mathbf{v}]_{i}
to denote the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
element of
\mathbf{v}
.

For general vectors
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
,
p(\mathbf{x})
denotes the probability density function of
\mathbf{x}
and
p(\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{y})
is the conditional probability density function of
\mathbf{x}
given
\mathbf{y}
. When
p(\mathbf{x})
is a (potentially multivariate) normal density, we will denote it by
f\left(\mathbf{x}\mid\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma}\right)
where
\bm{\mu}
is the mean vector and
\bm{\Sigma}
the covariance matrix, and we let
N(\mu,\sigma^{2})
denote a Gaussian random variable with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
.

1.3
Outline

In Section
2
, we introduce the VAMP and GVAMP iterations and provide intuition for their estimation schemes. In section
3
, we state our main concentration result, Theorem (
1
), and introduce the state evolution recursion which exactly characterizes the distributions of the limiting variables. In section
4
, we state and prove a concentration result for a more general abstract recursion and then prove Theorem (
1
) by showing how to recover the (G)VAMP estimates from this abstract recursion. In section
4.7
, we conclude with a discussion of future work.

2
Vector AMP and its Generalized Variant

In this section we discuss the VAMP algorithm and its generalized version, GVAMP. We focus on these two algorithms primarily, as the way that GAMP generalizes AMP is analogous, and our main analysis studies the most general of all these algorithms, GVAMP (from which one can derive results for GAMP and VAMP as well).
The VAMP iteration is similar to that of AMP in (
1.3
)-(
1.4
), and, like AMP, can be derived as a quadratic approximation to a belief propagation algorithm associated to a particular factor graph. The reader is referred to
[
36
]
for such a derivation. The GVAMP iteration extends the core ideas of the VAMP algorithm to handle arbitrary output distributions. Formally, GVAMP operates like two VAMP iterations glued together at the ends. Here, we provide some self-contained intuition for VAMP and GVAMP.

Algorithm
1
presents VAMP. We define a function
g_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
as

g_{2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\gamma_{2k})=(\gamma_{w}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\gamma_{w}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{y}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{r}_{2k}),

(2.1)

where
\gamma_{w}=1/\tau_{w}
and
\tau_{w}<\infty
is the elementwise variance of the noise
\mathbf{w}
. We assume that the noise variance
\tau_{w}
is known to simplify the analysis, though, practically VAMP can be run without such knowledge
[
20
]
. The function
g_{2}
in (
2.1
) has divergence with respect to the components of its first argument
\mathbf{r}_{2k}
given by

\mathrm{div}\left[g_{2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\gamma_{2k})\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial[\mathbf{r}_{2k}]_{i}}g_{2}([\mathbf{r}_{2k}]_{i},\gamma_{2k})=\gamma_{2k}\mathrm{Tr}\left((\gamma_{w}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\right),

where
\mathrm{Tr}(\cdot)
is the trace operator, i.e. it sum the diagonal elements of its argument.
Analogous to denoisers
g_{k}
in AMP, the function
g_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
taking input
(\mathbf{r}_{1k},\gamma_{1k})

must be specified by the user. In VAMP, however, we characterize the dependence of the denoiser on the iteration number through a parameter,
\gamma_{1k}>0
, and the function
g_{1}
itself does not change across iterations.

Algorithm 1

VAMP

1:

Number of iterations
K
, design matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
, observed
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, and denoiser
g_{1}(\cdot,\gamma_{1k})
. (Note:
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{y}
enter the algorithm through the definition of
g_{2}
in (
2.1
).)

2:

Initialize
\mathbf{r}_{10}
and
\gamma_{10}\geq 0
.

3:

for

k\leftarrow 0,\ldots,K

do

4:

\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}\leftarrow g_{1}(\mathbf{r}_{1k},\gamma_{1k}),

\alpha_{1k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[g_{1}(\mathbf{r}_{1k},\gamma_{1k})\right],

5:

\eta_{1k}\leftarrow\gamma_{1k}/\alpha_{1k},

\gamma_{2k}\leftarrow\eta_{1k}-\gamma_{1k},

6:

\mathbf{r}_{2k}\leftarrow\left(\eta_{1k}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}-\gamma_{1k}\mathbf{r}_{1k}\right)/\gamma_{2k},

7:

8:

\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2k}\leftarrow g_{2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\gamma_{2k}),

\alpha_{2k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[g_{2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\gamma_{2k})\right],

9:

\eta_{2k}\leftarrow\gamma_{2k}/\alpha_{2k},

\gamma_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\eta_{2k}-\gamma_{2k},

10:

\mathbf{r}_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\left(\eta_{2k}\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2k}-\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right)/\gamma_{1k}.

11:

end

for

The choice of
g_{1}
, and its relationship to
g_{2}
, can be better understood by carefully examining VAMP in the regression setting of (
1.2
)
with, specifically, Gaussian noise
\mathbf{w}\sim N\left(\mathbf{0},\tau_{w}\mathbf{I}_{M}\right)
.
As described above, we can view VAMP as trying to estimate some summary (e.g. the mode or mean) of the posterior distribution of the signal given the output. Recall that the posterior distribution is proportional to the product of the data likelihood and the signal prior, so in our case is given by

p(\mathbf{x}_{0}\mid\mathbf{y})\propto f\left(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0},\gamma_{w}^{-1}\mathbf{I}\right)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}p([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}),

(2.2)

where
f(\mathbf{y}\mid\bm{\mu},\mathbf{\Sigma})
is a multivariate normal density evaluated at
\mathbf{y}
having mean
\bm{\mu}
and covariance
\mathbf{\Sigma}
, so that
f\left(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0},\gamma_{w}^{-1}\mathbf{I}\right)
is the data likelihood in the case of Gaussian noise. In (
1.2
), we assume that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
has i.i.d. elements, thus
\prod_{i=1}^{N}p([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i})
is the signal prior distribution.

In general, the posterior in (
2.2
) is difficult to calculate; therefore, so is computing any summary statistic from it. For this reason, at each iteration, VAMP replaces the task of computing a posterior summary with two easier ones. First, observe that
f(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0},\gamma_{w}^{-1}\mathbf{I})
, as a function of
[\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}
, is proportional to a normal density
f([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}\mid[\mathbf{r}_{1}]_{i},[{\bm{\tau}}_{1}]_{i})
, for some mean
[\mathbf{r}_{1}]_{i}
and variance
[{\bm{\tau}}_{1}]_{i}
.

Making the further simplifying assumption that
[{\bm{\tau}}_{1}]_{i}
are equal across
i
, this yields the approximate posterior

p(\mathbf{x}_{0}\mid\mathbf{y})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 4.03563pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sim$}}}}{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 4.03563pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathrel{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 2.95741pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathrel{\raise 0.5382pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 2.18024pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}f(\mathbf{x}_{0}\mid\mathbf{r}_{1},\tau_{1}\mathbf{I})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}p([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}).

(2.3)

If we instead approximate the priors
p([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i})
by independent normal distributions with means
[\mathbf{r}_{2}]_{i}
and equal variances
\tau_{2}
, then we get the approximate posterior

p(\mathbf{x}_{0}\mid\mathbf{y})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\displaystyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 4.03563pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\sim$}}}}{\mathrel{\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\textstyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 4.03563pt\hbox{$\textstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathrel{\raise 0.75346pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 2.95741pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}}}{\mathrel{\raise 0.5382pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\propto$}\hss}\lower 2.18024pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}}}}f\left(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0},\gamma_{w}^{-1}\mathbf{I}\right)\cdot f\left(\mathbf{x}_{0}\mid\mathbf{r}_{2},\tau_{2}\mathbf{I}\right).

(2.4)

In each iteration, VAMP uses both approximate posteriors – (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) – to update the estimate of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
. Estimating
\mathbf{x}_{0}
by posterior (
2.3
) requires specifying
\mathbf{r}_{1}
and
\tau_{1}
, which control the approximate likelihood of the data. Since approximation (
2.4
) uses the true likelihood, VAMP uses the estimate from (
2.4
) to update
\mathbf{r}_{1}
and
\tau_{1}
in (
2.3
). Similarly, the estimates from (
2.3
) (which use the true prior) are used to update parameters
\mathbf{r}_{2}
and
\tau_{2}
for the approximate prior in (
2.4
). By iterating these steps, VAMP uses past estimates to improve both approximations, and then uses the improved approximations to further improve our estimates.

In this context,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
perform estimation of the two approximate models. The definitions of these functions depend on both the signal prior and desired posterior summary. In
(
2.4
)
, the prior is modeled as Gaussian regardless of the true prior. Since the resulting approximate posterior is again Gaussian, and the MMSE and MAP estimates are identical, (
2.1
) is the natural choice for
g_{2},
as it calculates the mean of (
2.4
). However, (
2.3
) depends on the choice of prior
p(\mathbf{x}_{0})
and the desired summary, so
g_{1}
must be specified by the user accordingly.

Next we turn to the GVAMP iteration. For GVAMP, the user must supply two denoising functions,
g_{x1}
and
g_{z1}
, because now both the prior and the output distributions are user-specified. Indeed, the function
g_{x1}
is meant to combine an estimate
\mathbf{r}_{2k}
of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
with the prior information
p(\mathbf{x}_{0})
to produce a new estimate of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
. Likewise, the function
g_{z1}
is meant to combine an estimate
\mathbf{p}_{2k}
of
\mathbf{z}_{0}
with the specified output distribution
p(y\mid z)
to produce an updated estimate of
\mathbf{z}_{0}
.

We define two other denoising functions in terms of the singular value decomposition
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{T}
with
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
,
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
orthogonal, and
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
the rectangular diagonal matrix of singular values. Let
g_{x2}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
g_{z2}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{M}
be defined as

\displaystyle g_{x2}\left(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}\mathbf{D}_{k}\left(\tau_{2k}\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{p}_{2k}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right),

(2.5)

\displaystyle g_{z2}\left(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{D}_{k}\left(\tau_{2k}\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{p}_{2k}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right).

(2.6)

where

\mathbf{D}_{k}=\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{d}_{k}),\text{ with }[\mathbf{d}_{k}]_{n}=\frac{1}{\tau_{2k}s_{n}^{2}+\gamma_{2k}}\text{ and }s_{n}=\begin{cases}[\mathbf{S}]_{nn},&n\leq M,\\
0,&M<n\leq N.\end{cases}

The divergences of these functions with respect to the components of
\mathbf{r}_{2k}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2k}
, respectively, are

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}[g_{x2}\left(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k}\right)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\gamma_{2k}}{\tau_{2k}s^{2}_{n}+\gamma_{2k}},\qquad\mathrm{div}[g_{z2}\left(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k}\right)]=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{\tau_{2k}s^{2}_{n}}{\tau_{2k}s^{2}_{n}+\gamma_{2k}}.

(2.7)

The above formulas follow because
\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B})=\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A})
when both products are defined. As for VAMP, the iteration dependence of the denoising is entirely controlled by parameters
\gamma_{2k}
and
\tau_{2k}
.

With this setup, we specify the GVAMP algorithm in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

GVAMP

1:

Number of iterations
K
, design matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
, observed
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, and denoisers
g_{x1}(\cdot,\gamma_{1k})
and
g_{z1}(\cdot,\tau_{1k})
. (Note:
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{y}
enter the algorithm through
g_{x2}
and
g_{z2}
in (
2.7
).)

2:

Initialize
\mathbf{r}_{11}
,
\mathbf{p}_{11}
,
\gamma_{11}\geq 0
, and
\tau_{11}\geq 0
.

3:

for

k\leftarrow 1,\ldots,K

do

4:

\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}\leftarrow g_{x1}(\mathbf{r}_{1k},\gamma_{1k}),

\alpha_{1k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[g_{x1}(\mathbf{r}_{1k},\gamma_{1k})\right],

5:

\mathbf{r}_{2k}\leftarrow\left(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}-\alpha_{1k}\mathbf{r}_{1k}\right)/(1-\alpha_{1k}),

\gamma_{2k}\leftarrow\gamma_{1k}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{1k}}-1\right),

6:

7:

\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{1k}\leftarrow g_{z1}(\mathbf{p}_{1k},\tau_{1k}),

\beta_{1k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[g_{z1}(\mathbf{p}_{1k},\tau_{1k})\right],

8:

\mathbf{p}_{2k}\leftarrow\left(\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{1k}-\beta_{1k}\mathbf{p}_{1k}\right)/(1-\beta_{1k}),

\tau_{2k}\leftarrow\tau_{1k}\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{1k}}-1\right),

9:

10:

\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2k}\leftarrow g_{x2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k}),

\alpha_{2k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[g_{x2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k})\right],

11:

\mathbf{r}_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\left(\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{2k}-\alpha_{2k}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right)/(1-\alpha_{2k}),

\gamma_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\gamma_{2k}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{2k}}-1\right),

12:

13:

\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{2k}\leftarrow g_{z2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k}),

\beta_{2k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[g_{z2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k})\right],

14:

\mathbf{p}_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\left(\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{2k}-\beta_{2k}\mathbf{p}_{2k}\right)/(1-\beta_{2k}),

\tau_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\tau_{2k}\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{2k}}-1\right).

15:

end

for

As in the case of VAMP, we can understand the GVAMP iteration as solving a series of simpler surrogate estimation problems that are combined between steps to yield an estimate of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
that accounts for all of the information in the full model. Each of these surrogate models incorporates the estimates from one or more of the other surrogate models by relating them to the parameters they are estimating through additive Gaussian noise. In other words, each of the surrogate models assume that
\mathbf{r}_{uk}=\mathbf{x}_{0}+\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{p}_{uk}=\mathbf{z}_{0}+\mathbf{w}
for
u\in\{1,2\}
where
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are i.i.d. mean
\mathbf{0}
Gaussian vectors with variances estimated by the model parameters
\gamma_{uk}
and
\tau_{uk}
.

The first surrogate model incorporates the prior with an estimate
\mathbf{r}_{1k}
of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
, having density

p_{1}(\mathbf{r}_{1k}\mid\gamma_{1k})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}p([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i})\,f([\mathbf{r}_{1k}]_{i}\mid[\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i},1/\gamma_{1k}),

(2.8)

where
f(\cdot\mid\mu,\sigma^{2})
is the normal density with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
. We can view
g_{x1}
as performing inference for the parameter
\mathbf{x}_{0}
of this model.
The second surrogate model combines estimates
\mathbf{r}_{2k}
of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2k}
of
\mathbf{z}_{0}
with the information from the full model encoded by the linear constraint
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}
. Its density is given as follows.

p_{2}(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k}\mid\gamma_{2k},\tau_{2k})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}f([\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}\mid[\mathbf{r}_{2k}]_{i},1/\gamma_{2k})\prod_{j=1}^{M}f([\mathbf{z}_{0}]_{j}\mid[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{j},1/\gamma_{w})\,f([\mathbf{p}_{2k}]_{j}\mid[\mathbf{z}_{0}]_{j},1/\tau_{2k}).

(2.9)

We can view
g_{x2}
and
g_{z2}
as performing inference for the parameters
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\mathbf{z}_{0}
of this model respectively in the limit as
\gamma_{w}\to\infty
(i.e. as the linear constraint is made exact).
The final surrogate model combines an estimate
\mathbf{p}_{1k}
of
\mathbf{z}_{0}
with the output distribution and has density

p_{3}(\mathbf{p}_{1k}\mid\tau_{1k})=\prod_{j=1}^{M}f([\mathbf{z}_{0}]_{j}\mid[\mathbf{p}_{1k}]_{j},1/\tau_{1k})\,p([\mathbf{y}]_{j}\mid[\mathbf{z}_{0}]_{j}).

(2.10)

The function
g_{z1}
can be seen as performing inference for the parameter
\mathbf{z}_{0}
of this model. The steps between the denoising steps in the GVAMP algorithm can viewed analogously to those in VAMP. In particular, they update the running estimates of the parameters
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\mathbf{z}_{0}
by combining the denoiser outputs with the previous estimates in a convex combination, with the relative weighting determined by the sensitivity (i.e. the divergence) of the denoiser at the previously denoised input.

3
Main Result

Before stating our main concentration result for GVAMP, from which results for VAMP and GAMP follow, we first revisit the notion of empirical convergence, we provide some more details on the matrix assumptions required for the VAMP algorithm to perform well, and we spend some time discussing the assumptions under which our main results follow.
First, we define the class of functions that act as test functions for assessing convergence and concentration.

Definition 1
.

A function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{J}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is pseudo-Lipschitz
of order
2
, denoted
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}(2)
, if for vectors
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
and some constant
L\geq 0
, it satisfies

\left|\phi(\mathbf{v})-\phi(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\right|\leq L\|\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|\left(1+\|\mathbf{v}\|+\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|\right).

With this concept, we can then define empirical convergence and concentration of vector sequences to
L^{2}
random variables/distributions in the following way.

Definition 2

(Empirical convergence)
.

We will say that a sequence of random vectors
\{\mathbf{v}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{J}
converges empirically (with
2
nd moment) to
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
if
\mathbb{E}\{[\mathbf{V}_{j}]^{2}\}<\infty
for all
1\leq j\leq J
and if

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\phi(\mathbf{v}_{n})\to\mathbb{E}\{\phi(\mathbf{V})\},

almost surely for any
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}(2):\mathbb{R}^{J}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 3

(Exponentially fast concentration)
.

We will say that a random vector sequence
\{\mathbf{v}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{J}
concentrates exponentially fast (in
N
) on
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
if
\mathbb{E}\{[\mathbf{V}_{j}]^{2}\}<\infty
for all
1\leq j\leq J
, and if for all
\epsilon>0
and all
N\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\phi(\mathbf{v}_{n})-\mathbb{E}\phi(\mathbf{V})\Big|>\epsilon\Big)\leq K\exp(-kN\epsilon^{2}),

for any
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}(2):\mathbb{R}^{J}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
K,k>0
are universal constants independent of
\phi
and
N
.

If, for any finite
N\geq 0
,
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{N}
are drawn i.i.d. from a subgaussian distribution, then
[
38
, Lemma B.4]
shows that
\{\mathbf{v}_{n}\}
concentrates on
\mathbf{v}_{1}
. Thus, this definition roughly requires the sequence
\{\mathbf{v}_{n}\}
to behave asymptotically like an i.i.d. sample from a subgaussian distribution.
In what follows, we simply write that a vector sequence converges empirically to or concentrates exponentially fast on some random variable, without explicitly stating that the random variable has a finite
2
nd moment.

The following definitions allow us to characterize those matrices
\mathbf{A}
to which our results apply.

Definition 4

(Haar Distribution)
.

A matrix
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
is Haar distributed on the class of
N\times N
orthogonal matrices if
\mathbf{V}_{0}\mathbf{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{V}
for any non-random orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{V}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
.

Definition 5

(Orthogonal Invariance)
.

For a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
, let
\mathbf{s}
be its vector of singular values and let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{T}
be its singular value decomposition, where
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
and
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
are orthogonal matrices and
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
is the rectangular, diagonal matrix with
\mathbf{S}_{ii}=\mathbf{s}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq M
.
\mathbf{A}
is
orthogonally invariant
if
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}
are independent and
Haar distributed
on the groups of
N\times N
and
M\times M
orthogonal matrices, respectively. This implies that the distributions of
\mathbf{U}_{0}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{V}_{0}
for any fixed orthogonal
\mathbf{U}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
and
\mathbf{V}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
and
\mathbf{A}
are identical.

3.1
Assumptions for the Main Results

Now we state the assumptions under which we provide our main result for the GVAMP algorithm. We can remove some of these assumptions for the finite sample analysis of VAMP (see Remark
1
).

Assumption 0.
The initial estimates are generated as
\mathbf{r}_{10}=\mathbf{V}\mathbf{r}^{\mathrm{init}}
and
\mathbf{p}_{10}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{init}}
for vectors
\mathbf{r}^{\mathrm{init}}
and
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{init}}
with i.i.d. subgaussian entries and independent of the matrices
\mathbf{V}
,
\mathbf{U}
. The output vector
\mathbf{y}
can be expressed as
\mathbf{y}=h\left(\mathbf{z}_{0},\mathbf{w}\right)
where
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}
is the transformed input,
\mathbf{w}
is an independent “disturbance” vector with i.i.d. subgaussian components, and
h
is a measurable function. We note that this function is also implicitly constrained by the Lipschitz assumption on
g_{z1}
below since this denoiser leverages the likelihood
f(y=h(z,w)\mid\cdot)
for estimating
\mathbf{z}_{0}
.

Next, the truth
\mathbf{x}_{0}
is also independent of these other quantities and has i.i.d. subgaussian components. These conditions imply that
\mathbf{x}_{0}
,
\mathbf{w}
, and the initial estimates
\mathbf{r}_{10}
and
\mathbf{p}_{10}
jointly concentrate on a random vector
(X_{0},W,R_{10},P_{10})
by Lemma
18
. Our main technical result Lemma
5
will also imply that
\mathbf{z}_{0}
concentrate on a Gaussian random variable
Z_{0}
.

The entries of the singular value vector
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}
are sampled i.i.d. from a distribution supported in a bounded interval
[0,s_{\max}]
, where the upper bound
s_{\max}
is independent of
N
.
Furthermore,
\mathbf{s}
is independent of the singular vector matrices
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
. The concentration assumption for
\mathbf{x}_{0}
is satisfied whenever the components of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
are drawn independently from a subgaussian distribution.

Furthermore, the initial precision estimates
\gamma_{10}
and
\tau_{10}
are positive for all
N
and converge to some
\overline{\gamma}_{10},\overline{\tau}_{10}>0
, respectively. Finally, we assume a fixed sparsity level
\delta=\frac{M}{N}
independent of
N
.

Assumption 1.
The design matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
is orthogonally invariant. If
\mathbf{A}
has singular value decomposition
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{V}^{T}
, then this is equivalent to
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}
being independently Haar distributed on the group of
N\times N
and
M\times M
orthogonal matrices, denoted
\textsf{O}(N)
and
\textsf{O}(M)
, respectively. The Haar property means that
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}^{\prime}\mathbf{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{U}
for any other
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\in\textsf{O}(N)
and
\mathbf{U}^{\prime}\in\textsf{O}(M)
. Definitions of right orthogonal invariance and the Haar distribution along with some useful properties of these kinds of random elements are given in Definition
4
.

To obtain concentration for VAMP, it suffices to assume that
\mathbf{A}
is just right orthogonally invariant. This only makes the Haar assumption on
\mathbf{V}
, and
\mathbf{U}
may be an arbitrary orthogonal matrix. (In particular, no independence needs to be assumed between
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}
.)

Assumption 2.

The log prior
\log p(x)
and the log likelihood
\log p(y\mid z)
(as a function of
z
) are concave and
\beta
-smooth. The latter property just requires that, for some
\beta>0
,

-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\log p(x)<\beta,\qquad-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\log p(y\mid z)<\beta.

(3.1)

Assumption 3.
The estimating functions
g_{x1}
,
g_{z1}
,
g_{x2}
, and
g_{z2}
are separable
1
1

1

A function
g:\mathbb{R}^{J}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{J}
is
separable
if for
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist a function
\widetilde{g}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
for which
[g(\mathbf{v},z)]_{j}=\widetilde{g}([\mathbf{v}]_{j},z)
for all
j\in[J]
.
, and both
g_{x1}
and
g_{z1}
and their derivatives are uniformly Lipschitz
2
2

2

A function
\phi(\mathbf{w},c)
is uniformly Lipschitz at
c_{0}
if there is an open neighborhood
U
of
c_{0}
and a constant
L>0
such that
\phi(\cdot,c)
is
L-
Lipschitz for any fixed
c\in U
and
\left|\phi(\mathbf{w},c_{1})-\phi(\mathbf{w},c_{2})\right|\leq L\left(1+\|\mathbf{w}\|\right)\left|c_{1}-c_{2}\right|
for all
c_{1},c_{2}\in U
.
at
\overline{\gamma}_{1k}
for all
k\geq 0
. When
g_{x1}
and
g_{z1}
are the MAP or MMSE estimators for the models (
2.3
), they are separable. Henceforth, we will slightly abuse notation by writing
these functions as taking both vector and scalar input. For VAMP, these same assumptions apply to the estimating functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
.

Assumption 4.
The
\alpha_{ik}
and
\beta_{ik}
are truncated to lie in some interval
[t_{\min},t_{\max}]\subset(0,1)
. The
\gamma_{ik}
and
\tau_{ik}
are also clipped so that they lie in some intervals
[\gamma_{\min},\gamma_{\max}]
with
0<\gamma_{\min}<\gamma_{\max}<\infty
and
[\tau_{\min},\tau_{\max}]
with
0<\tau_{\min}<\tau_{\max}<\infty
respectively.
Intuitively, this prevents the
\mathbf{r}
and
\mathbf{p}
updates from blowing up and thus assists with convergence. From the point of view of concentration, the truncation allows us to control the exponential rate of convergence. Truncating the
\alpha_{ik}
and
\beta_{ik}
is not necessary if the second derivatives in (
3.1
) are also lower bounded by some
\alpha>0
(in which case the densities are also
\alpha
-strongly log-concave).

Assumption 5.
We terminate the algorithm according to stopping criteria, given explicitly in the next section. Essentially they imply that we stop if the MSE of our current estimate is sufficiently small or if there is a sufficiently small change in the estimate between successive iterations.

Remark 1
.

For VAMP, some simplifications of the above assumptions are possible. Specifically, in Assumption 1, we only need
\mathbf{V}
to be Haar distributed (
\mathbf{U}
can be treated as deterministic), and in Assumption 2, the latter inequality in (
3.1
) is automatic (since the output is normal by definition).

Remark 2
.

We note that these assumptions do exclude our analysis from applying to (G)VAMP using a denoiser
g_{1}
having a derivative that is piecewise constant and, thus, not uniformly Lipschitz, like the soft-thresholding function. However, if the derivative is bounded (like in the case of the soft-thresholding function), the analysis will still apply to appropriately constructed smooth approximations to the function that would provide qualitatively similar inference.

Remark 3
.

The requirement of
Assumption 0
that
(\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{r}_{10},\mathbf{p}_{10})
jointly concentrate exponentially fast holds if
\mathbf{r}^{\mathrm{init}}
and
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{init}}
are i.i.d. sampled from a subgaussian distribution and are independent of each other and of all other quantities. This follows by Lemmas
17
and
18
as well as the assumed concentration of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and the assumed independence of
\mathbf{x}_{0}
,
\mathbf{U}
, and
\mathbf{V}
.

Remark 4
.

The assumption of fixed sparsity level
\delta
can be relaxed to allow for sequences
\delta_{N}=\frac{M}{N}
, bounded away from
0
and converging to some limit
\delta_{N}\to\delta>0
as
N\to\infty
. However, this requires introducing an
N
dependence to the state evolution, adding notational and conceptual complexity.

Remark 5
.

If we make the additional assumption that
\mathbb{P}(S=0)=0
(i.e. that
\mathbf{A}
has full rank with probability one), then can express the SVD of
\mathbf{A}
as
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}_{M}\mathbf{V}^{T}_{M}
where
\mathbf{S}_{M}
and
\mathbf{V}_{M}
are the matrices obtained from
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{V}
by retaining only the first
M
columns, so that
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
and
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times M}
. This “economy” decomposition is unique up to permutations of the singular values/vectors with probability one, so we have that
p(\mathbf{A})\propto p(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}_{M},\mathbf{s})=p(\mathbf{U})p(\mathbf{V}_{M})p(\mathbf{s})
.
It follows from symmetry properties of the Haar measure that the distribution of
\mathbf{V}_{M}
is uniform over
N\times M
matrices with orthonormal columns and, for any singular value bound
s_{\max}<\infty
, we are free within our assumptions to take
[\mathbf{s}]_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}\mathsf{uniform}\left([0,s_{\max}]\right)
(as all bounded variables exhibit subgaussian tail concentration). With this choice, the right-hand side above becomes constant; thus, our assumptions are broad enough to apply to the uniform distribution over the class of full rank matrices
\mathbf{A}
with singular values drawn from
(0,s_{\max}]^{M}
.

For VAMP, our assumptions are only slightly more restrictive than those used for the asymptotic analyses in
[
36
]
, with the main differences being that we assume (i) exponentially fast concentration rather than convergence of input quantities and (ii) clipping and truncating of scalar iterates in Assumption 4 (though,
[
36
]
also suggest clipping
\gamma
as a practical matter of algorithm stability).

3.2
Main Results

Rangan
et al.

[
36
]
show empirical convergence of the vector sequence
\left\{\left([\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}]_{i},[\mathbf{r}_{1k}]_{i},[\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{N}

to
(\hat{X}_{1k},R_{1k},X_{0})
for the case of the VAMP iterates where
R_{1k}=X_{0}+\sqrt{\tau_{1k}}Z
with
\hat{X}_{1k}=g_{1}(R_{1k},\overline{\gamma}_{1k})
and
Z\sim N(0,1)
independent of
X_{0}\sim p(\mathbf{x}_{0})
.
The constants
\tau_{1k}
and
\overline{\gamma}_{1k}
that describe the limiting variable
\hat{X}_{1k}
can be characterized exactly by the state evolution equations for VAMP, which we turn our attention to in Section
3.3
after we present our main result, Theorem
1
, characterizing the asymptotic rate of this empirical convergence for VAMP and GVAMP.

Theorem 1
.

[VAMP and GVAMP Concentration.]
Under
Assumption 0
-
Assumption 5
given above, for any
\phi\in\mathrm{PL}(2):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, any
k\geq 0
, and any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi([\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}]_{i},[\mathbf{x}_{0}]_{i})-\mathbb{E}\{\phi(\hat{X}_{1k},X_{0})\}\Big|\geq\epsilon\Big)\leq C^{\prime}C_{k}e^{-c^{\prime}c_{k}N\epsilon^{2}},

(3.2)

where
C_{k}=C^{2k}(k!)^{16}
and
c_{k}=\frac{1}{c^{2k}(k!)^{22}}
with
C,C^{\prime}
and
c,c^{\prime}
being universal constants not depending on
\epsilon
or
N
and
\hat{X}_{1k}=g_{1}(R_{1k},\overline{\gamma}_{1k})
where
R_{1k}=X_{0}+\sigma_{1k}Z
with
Z\sim N(0,1)
independent of
X_{0}
. The constants
\sigma_{1k}
and
\overline{\gamma}_{1k}
are defined in Eq. (
3.5
) in Section
3.3
. In (
3.2
), the vector
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}
is that given in either Algorithm
2
or Algorithm
1
.

Remark 6
.

We emphasize that Theorem
1
is true for both GVAMP (Algorithm
2
) and VAMP (Algorithm
1
), while the assumptions can be slightly simplified for VAMP as discussed in Remark
1
. This is because moving from proving VAMP concentration to proving GVAMP concentration requires the addition of an ’output’ channel (whereas as the ’input’ channel is analyzed in both cases). The analysis of the ’output’ channel is symmetric to that of the input’ channel. There is a technical difficulty in that GVAMP requires analyzing denoising functions that depend on the vector
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}
, whereas a direct analysis of the VAMP algorithm would not need to consider this, but our theoretical analysis of the GVAMP algorithm below still implies the equivalent result for the (simplified) VAMP algorithm.

The proof of Theorem
1
is given in Section
4.6
. The key to proving Theorem 1 comes in Lemma
4
, which establishes that the GVAMP iterates are jointly equal in distribution to the iterates of another recursion defined entirely in terms of functions of i.i.d. Gaussian variables. The proof strategy for our main technical concentration lemma (Lemma
6
, which implies Theorem
1
) then boils down to using the theory concentration for transformations of Gaussians to inductively establish the desired results for this distributionally equivalent recursion.

We finally mention that the proof of Theorem
1
implies a similar concentration result for GAMP as well. This is because, conceptually, we have seen that upgrading the finite sample analysis of an AMP-style algorithm (VAMP) to its generalized version (GVAMP) only requires repeating the same analysis for a symmetric ’output’ channel and including a few more assumptions to deal with dependencies between the measurement matrix in the algorithm and the dependence of the denoiser on the output. Hence, GAMP concentration follows from the proof in
[
38
]
.

To save space and to avoid introducing further notation, we only state an informal theorem of this result here, and we refer to the statement of the GAMP algorithm in
[
17
, Section 4 Equation (55)]
and its corresponding state evolution in
[
17
, Section 4 Equation (57)-(58)]
.

Theorem 2
.

[Informal GAMP Concentration.]
Considering the GAMP algorithm in
[
17
, Section 4 Equation (55)]
and its state evolution in
[
17
, Section 4 Equation (57)-(58)]
, the asymptotic equivalence of its iterates and its state evolution (see for example for an asymptotic statement in
[
17
, Section 4 Theorem 4.2]
) can be upgraded to exponentially fast concentration with rates of
CC_{k}e^{-cc_{k}N\epsilon^{2}}
where
C_{k}=C^{2k}(k!)^{\kappa}
and
c_{k}=\frac{1}{c^{2k}(k!)^{\kappa^{\prime}}}
with
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
and
c,c^{\prime}
being universal constants not depending on
\epsilon
or
N
. While we do not specify
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
explicitly in this informal statement, these will be small constant values (like
\kappa=16
and
\kappa^{\prime}=22
from Theorem
1
).

The assumptions for this result are slightly different than for GVAMP. In particular, all assumptions about the matrix
\mathbf{A}
above are replaced by the assumption that
\mathbf{A}
has i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries (the same condition under which the large system analysis of GAMP was originally given). Furthermore, the initialization may now be randomly generated without the additional multiplication by
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}
(as in
Assumption 0
). The remaining assumptions are analogous to those given above, and these represent natural extensions of the assumptions used for original large system analysis of GAMP. In particular, the Lipschitz condition on the denoisers must be extended to the uniformly Lipschitz condition in order to control the rate of change in the
\tau
parameters, the initial data and disturbance vector
\mathbf{w}
must now be drawn i.i.d. from subgaussian distributions in order to ensure concentration at the appropriate rate, and stronger control is needed over the denoiser derivatives in order to control the probability of algorithm divergence.

3.3
State Evolution

As mentioned previously, state evolution equations describe the dynamics of AMP-style algorithms. Generally, these are recursive relationships that characterize the effective signal-to-noise ratios at various stages of the algorithm.
To specify the GVAMP state evolution, which provides the constants in the expectation in Theorem
1
we must define sensitivity and error functions for each of the approximate models (
2.8
), (
2.9
), and (
2.10
). Following
[
36
]
, the sensitivity functions are

\displaystyle A_{x1}(\gamma_{1},\sigma^{2}_{1})=\mathbb{E}\{g_{x1}^{\prime}(R_{1},\gamma_{1})\},\quad

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad A_{x2}(\gamma_{2},\tau_{2})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\gamma_{2}}{N}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\tau_{2}\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{S}+\gamma_{2}\right)^{-1}\right],

(3.3)

\displaystyle A_{z1}(\tau_{1},\rho^{2}_{1})=\mathbb{E}\{g_{z1}^{\prime}(P_{1},\tau_{1})\},\quad

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad A_{z2}(\gamma_{2},\tau_{2})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\tau_{2}}{M}\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{S})\left(\tau_{2}\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{S}+\gamma_{2}\right)^{-1}\right],

(3.4)

where
R_{1}\sim N(X_{0},\sigma^{2}_{1})
and
P_{1}\sim N(Z_{0},\rho^{2}_{1})
and the derivatives of
g_{x1}
and
g_{z1}
are taken with respect to their first input. Next, the error functions are defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{x1}(\gamma_{1},\sigma^{2}_{1})=\mathbb{E}[\left(g_{x1}(R_{1},\gamma_{1})-X_{0}\right)^{2}],\,

\displaystyle\text{ and }\,\mathcal{E}_{x2}(\gamma_{2},\tau_{2},\sigma^{2}_{2},\rho^{2}_{2})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\left\|g_{x2}(\mathbf{r}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{2},\gamma_{2},\tau_{2})-\mathbf{x}_{0}\right\|^{2}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{z1}(\tau_{1},\rho^{2}_{1})=\mathbb{E}[\left(g_{z1}(P_{1},\tau_{1})-Z_{0}\right)^{2}],\,

\displaystyle\text{ and }\,\mathcal{E}_{z2}(\gamma_{2},\tau_{2},\sigma^{2}_{2},\rho^{2}_{2})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}[\left\|g_{z2}(\mathbf{r}_{2},\mathbf{p}_{2},\gamma_{2},\tau_{2})-\mathbf{z}_{0}\right\|^{2}],

where
\mathbf{r}_{2}\sim N(\mathbf{x}_{0},\sigma^{2}_{2}\mathbf{I})
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}\sim N(\mathbf{z}_{0},\rho^{2}_{2}\mathbf{I})
, and where
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}
.

In terms of these functions, we define the scalar evolution equations for GVAMP. This sequence of equations determines the constants used in expectation in Theorem
1
. Initializations
\overline{\gamma}_{10}
and
\overline{\tau}_{10}
are defined in
Assumption 0
as limits of the precision inputs to the GVAMP algorithm, and we initialize the
\sigma
and
\rho
terms as
\sigma^{2}_{10}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(R_{10}-X_{0}\right)^{2}\right\}
and
\rho^{2}_{10}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(P_{10}-Z_{0}\right)^{2}\right\}
where the random variables
X_{0},Z_{0},R_{10},
and
P_{10}
are defined in
Assumption 0
. Then for
k\geq 0
,

\begin{split}\overline{\alpha}_{1k}&=A_{x1}(\overline{\gamma}_{1k},\sigma^{2}_{1k}),\\
\sigma^{2}_{2k}&=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{x1}(\overline{\gamma}_{1k},\sigma^{2}_{1k})-\overline{\alpha}_{1k}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{1k}}{(1-\overline{\alpha}_{1k})^{2}},\\
\overline{\gamma}_{2k}&=\overline{\gamma}_{1k}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{\alpha}_{1k}}-1\Big),\\
\overline{\alpha}_{2k}&=A_{x2}(\overline{\gamma}_{2k},\overline{\tau}_{2k}),\\
\sigma^{2}_{1(k+1)}&=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{x2}(\overline{\gamma}_{2k},\overline{\tau}_{2k},\rho^{2}_{2k},\sigma^{2}_{2k})-\overline{\alpha}_{2k}^{2}\sigma^{2}_{2k}}{(1-\overline{\alpha}_{2k})^{2}},\\
\overline{\gamma}_{1(k+1)}&=\overline{\gamma}_{2k}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{\alpha}_{2k}}-1\Big),\end{split}\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{split}\overline{\beta}_{1k}&=A_{z1}(\overline{\tau}_{1k},\sigma^{2}_{2k}),\\
\rho^{2}_{2k}&=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{z1}(\overline{\tau}_{1k},\rho^{2}_{1k})-\overline{\beta}_{1k}^{2}\rho^{2}_{1k}}{(1-\overline{\beta}_{1k})^{2}},\\
\overline{\tau}_{2k}&=\overline{\tau}_{1k}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{\beta}_{1k}}-1\Big),\\
\overline{\beta}_{2k}&=A_{z2}(\overline{\gamma}_{2k},\overline{\tau}_{2k}),\\
\rho^{2}_{1(k+1)}&=\frac{\mathcal{E}_{z2}(\overline{\gamma}_{2k},\overline{\tau}_{2k},\rho^{2}_{2k},\sigma^{2}_{2k})-\overline{\beta}_{2k}^{2}\rho^{2}_{2k}}{(1-\overline{\beta}_{2k})^{2}},\\
\overline{\tau}_{1(k+1)}&=\overline{\tau}_{2k}\Big(\frac{1}{\overline{\beta}_{2k}}-1\Big).\end{split}

(3.5)

This state evolution closely resembles the state evolution for VAMP derived in
[
36
]
, with additional terms to account for the added complexity of the output distribution. In short, the VAMP state evolution is structurally the same recursion with only half the terms.

4
A General Concentration Result

Theorem
1
is a consequence of a concentration result for a more general iteration, Algorithm
3
, which we introduce now. Let
f_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}(p_{1},p_{2},w_{p}^{(1)},w_{p}^{(2)},\tau,\gamma):\mathbb{R}^{2d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a function of
2d+2
arguments and two parameters, and let
f_{p}^{\mathrm{out}}
,
f_{q}^{\mathrm{in}}
, and
f_{q}^{\mathrm{out}}
be defined similarly. Furthermore let
\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}(\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p},\tau,\gamma):\mathbb{R}^{2d\times N}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\times N}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
be given by applying
f_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}
separably over the
N
rows of its arguments.

We note that if
M<N
, then
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
and
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{out}}_{k}
(defined in Algorithm
3
below) have different numbers of columns. When this occurs, we must take care in defining the notion of separability. In general, we will encounter situations in which we want to extend a function
\phi(x,y,w):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\in PL(2)
of
2d+2
arguments
x,y
and
w
separably over vectorized inputs
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times M}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times N}
or
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times M}
for which
M\leq N
.
When
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times M}
, define the separable extension by simply truncating
\mathbf{x}
to an
d\times M
matrix, and the resulting extended function takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
. When
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times N}
, it will suffice for our purposes to consider only those
\phi
for which
\phi([\mathbf{x}]_{i},[\mathbf{y}]_{i},[\mathbf{w}]_{i})
is independent of
y
for all
i>M
. In this case, the separable extension of
\phi
over these vectorized arguments clearly makes sense. By convention, unless otherwise noted, we take
[\mathbf{y}]_{i}=\mathbf{0}
for
i>M
so that expressions like
\phi([\mathbf{x}]_{i},[\mathbf{y}]_{i},[\mathbf{w}]_{i})
are well-defined for all
i\leq N
. In particular, we use these conventions to define separable extensions of the functions
\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\tau,\gamma)
and
\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\tau,\gamma)
over vectorized arguments/parameters in the following sections.

Now, letting
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}
be orthogonal matrices, we can state Algorithm
3
which defines a recursion in terms of these quantities.

Algorithm 3

General Recursion for GVAMP

1:

Orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
and
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
; separable denoisers
\mathbf{f}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}
,
\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{out}}
,
\mathbf{f}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}
, and
\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{out}}
(defined over their argument according to the conventions given above);
parameter update functions
\Gamma_{p/q}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and disturbance vectors
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times N}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times M}
.

2:

Initialize
\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
,
\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times d}
,
\gamma_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\gamma_{pk}^{\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

3:

for

k\leftarrow 0,\ldots,K

do

4:

\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{V}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}
,
\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}\leftarrow\mathbf{U}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}
,

5:

\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\right)\right]
,
\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{out}}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{out}}\left(\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk},\gamma_{pk}\right)\right]
,

6:

[\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}\leftarrow\frac{1}{1-[\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}}\left[\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\right)_{j}-[\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}[\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}\right]
,

[\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}\leftarrow\frac{1}{1-[\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}}\left[\mathbf{f}_{p}^{\mathrm{out}}\left(\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\right)_{j}-[\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}[\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}\right]
.

7:

8:

\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}\leftarrow\Gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}(\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk},\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}})
,
\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}\leftarrow\Gamma_{q}^{\mathrm{out}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk},\alpha_{pk}^{\mathrm{out}})
,

9:

\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}\leftarrow\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}
,
\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}\leftarrow\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}
,

10:

\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}\right)\right]
,
\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}\left[\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{out}}\left(\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}\right)\right]
,

11:

[\mathbf{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}\leftarrow\frac{1}{1-[\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}}\left[\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}\right)_{j}-[\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}[\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{j}\right]
,

[\mathbf{u}_{k+1}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}\leftarrow\frac{1}{1-[\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}}\left[\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{out}}\left(\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}\right)_{j}-[\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}[\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{j}\right]
.

12:

\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{p(k+1)}\leftarrow\Gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}(\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk},\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}})
,
\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{p(k+1)}\leftarrow\Gamma_{p}^{\mathrm{out}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk},\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}})
,

13:

end

for

Likewise, the VAMP iterates can be generated from a simpler general recursion given in Algorithm
4
. The VAMP algorithm is not strictly a special case of GVAMP; similarly, Algorithm
4
is not a special case of Algorithm
3
. However, Algorithm
4
can be seen as a subset of Algorithm
3
by discarding all the “output” steps in Algorithm
3
and making the denoisers
f_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
f_{q}^{\mathrm{in}}
depend only on the input variables. The resulting “input only” algorithm exactly recovers Algorithm
4
.

Algorithm 4

General Recursion for VAMP

1:

Orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
, separable (w.r.t. the first two arguments) denoisers

f_{p}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2\times N}\text{ and }f_{q}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}\times\mathbb{R}^{2\times N}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N},

divergence functions
C_{i}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, parameter update functions
\Gamma_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and disturbance vectors
\mathbf{w}^{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\mathbf{w}^{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
.

2:

Initialize
\mathbf{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

3:

for

k\leftarrow 0,\ldots,K

do

4:

\mathbf{p}_{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{V}\mathbf{u}_{k},

\alpha_{1k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}[f_{p}(\mathbf{p}_{k},\mathbf{w}^{p},\gamma_{1k})],

5:

\mathbf{v}_{k}\leftarrow C_{1}(\alpha_{1k})\left[f_{p}(\mathbf{p}_{k},\mathbf{w}^{p},\gamma_{1k})-\alpha_{1k}\mathbf{p}_{k}\right],

\gamma_{2k}\leftarrow\Gamma_{1}\left(\gamma_{1k},\alpha_{1k}\right),

6:

7:

\mathbf{q}_{k}\leftarrow\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{v}_{k},

\alpha_{2k}\leftarrow\mathrm{div}[f_{q}(\mathbf{q}_{k},\mathbf{w}^{q},\gamma_{2k})],

8:

\mathbf{u}_{k+1}\leftarrow C_{2}(\alpha_{2k})\left[f_{q}(\mathbf{q}_{k},\mathbf{w}^{q},\gamma_{2k})-\alpha_{2k}\mathbf{q}_{k}\right],

\gamma_{1(k+1)}\leftarrow\Gamma_{2}\left(\gamma_{2k},\alpha_{2k}\right).

9:

end

for

As a result, all of the arguments regarding concentration of the iterates in Algorithm
3
can be specialized to arguments for concentration of the iterates of Algorithm
4
. Henceforth, unless specified otherwise, all arguments and discussion will focus on Algorithm
3
. To recover the relevant statements for Algorithm
4
, it suffices to drop the “
\mathrm{in}
” and “
\mathrm{out}
” superscripts (and, when both occur, eliminate dependence on the “
\mathrm{out}
” variable).

We note that the divergence terms
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in Algorithm
3
are computed component-wise, so e.g.

[\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{\mathrm{in}}_{j}}\left[f_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(p_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\ldots,p_{d}^{\mathrm{in}},p^{\mathrm{out}}_{1},\ldots,p^{\mathrm{out}}_{d},w_{p},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}\right)\right]_{j}\;\Bigg|_{\left([\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]_{i},[\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{out}}_{k}]_{i},[\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}]_{i},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{p}k\right)},

for each
j=1,\ldots,d
.

We establish a correspondence between the general algorithm, Algorithm
3
, and GVAMP in Algorithm
2
, through the following translation. We take the orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
in Algorithm
3
to be the left and right singular vector matrices of
\mathbf{A}
, respectively. We make the following definitions:

\begin{split}\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}=\left[\mathbf{r}_{1k}\;\bm{0}\right],\quad\qquad\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}=\left[\mathbf{p}_{1k}\;\mathbf{z}_{0},\right],&\quad\qquad\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}=\left[\mathbf{r}_{2k}\;,\mathbf{x}_{0}\right],\quad\qquad\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}=\left[\mathbf{p}_{2k},\;\bm{0}\right],\\
\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}=\left[\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{2k},\;\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{0}\right],\quad\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}=\left[\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{p}_{2k},\;\bm{0}\right],&\quad\qquad\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}=\left[\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{1k},\;\bm{0}\right],\quad\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}=\left[\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{p}_{1k},\;\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{z}_{0}\right].\end{split}

(4.1)

Furthermore, we make the following function definitions:

\begin{split}f_{p}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(p^{\mathrm{in}},p^{\mathrm{out}},w_{p},\tau,\gamma\right)&=\left[g_{x1}\left([p^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1},\gamma\right),\;[w_{p}]_{1}\right],\\
f_{p}^{\mathrm{out}}\left(p^{\mathrm{in}},p^{\mathrm{out}},w_{p},\tau,\gamma\right)&=\left[g_{z1}\left([p^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1},\tau,h\left([p^{\mathrm{out}}]_{2},[w_{p}]_{1}\right)\right),\;0\right],\\
f_{q}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(q^{\mathrm{in}},q^{\mathrm{out}},w_{q},\tau,\gamma\right)&=\left[\left([\tau]_{1}[w_{q}]_{1}[q^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1}+[\gamma]_{1}[q^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}\right)/\left([\tau]_{1}[w_{q}]_{1}^{2}+[\gamma]_{1}\right)\;,0\right],\\
f_{q}^{\mathrm{out}}\left(q^{\mathrm{in}},q^{\mathrm{out}},w_{q},\tau,\gamma\right)&=\left[[w_{q}]_{1}\left([\tau]_{1}[w_{q}]_{1}[q^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1}+[\gamma]_{1}[q^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}\right)/\left([\tau]_{1}[w_{q}]_{1}^{2}+[\gamma]_{1}\right),\;[w_{q}]_{1}[q^{\mathrm{in}}]_{2}\right].\end{split}

(4.2)

Finally we define disturbance vectors and constants:

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}=\mathbf{x}_{0},\qquad\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p}=\mathbf{w},\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}=[(\mathbf{s}\;\bm{0})\;\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}],\qquad\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}=[\mathbf{s}\;\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}],

(4.3)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}=\left(\alpha_{1k},0\right),\qquad\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}=\left(\alpha_{2k},0\right),\qquad

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}=\left(\beta_{1k},0\right),\qquad\alpha^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}=\left(\beta_{2k},0\right),

(4.4)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}=(\gamma_{1k},1),\qquad\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}=(\gamma_{2k},1),\qquad

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}=(\tau_{1k},1),\qquad\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}=(\tau_{2k},1).

(4.5)

In (
4.3
),
(\mathbf{s}\;\bm{0})
is the
N
-vector obtained by padding the positive singular values of
\mathbf{A}
with
0
’s, and
[\mathbf{s}\;\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}]
is the
M\times 2
matrix with columns
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{u}_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}
. Keeping with our matrix notation,
[\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p}]_{i}
will denote column
i
of this matrix, i.e. the vector
([\mathbf{z}_{0}]_{i},[\mathbf{y}]_{i})
. In all cases, where we perform truncation of the scalar quantities in GVAMP, we assume the same truncation here. We claim that these quantities satisfy the recursion in Algorithm
3
. We start by establishing this for the scalar terms inductively. For the
\gamma
terms, if equality has been established for all scalar terms up to the current term, then equality follows for the current term immediately if we define the
\Gamma
functions as follows:

\Gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}(\gamma,\alpha)=\Gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{p}(\gamma,\alpha)=\Gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}(\gamma,\alpha)=\Gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}(\gamma,\alpha)=\left(\frac{\gamma_{1}(1-\alpha_{1})}{\alpha_{1}},1\right).

For the
\alpha
terms, we first observe that the equality follows immediately for
\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}
given the definitions of
f^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}
and
f^{\mathrm{out}}_{p}
respectively (assuming equality for all prior scalar terms). It follows directly from the definitions of
f^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}
and
f^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}
that

[\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}]_{1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{[\gamma_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}}{[\gamma_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1}[\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}]^{2}_{i1}+\gamma_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}}}_{1},\qquad[\alpha_{qk}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{[\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}]_{i1}^{2}[\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}]_{1}}{[\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}]_{1}[\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}]_{i1}^{2}+[\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}]_{1}}.

Substituting
(\mathbf{s}\;\bm{0})
for
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}
and
\mathbf{s}
for
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}
, it follows that these are exactly the divergences of
g_{x2}
and
g_{z2}
derived in
[
40
]
, and hence are equal to
\alpha_{2k}
and
\beta_{2k}
, respectively.

Observe that lines
1
and
4
in Algorithm
3
follow trivially from the definitions in (
4.1
) and that

\displaystyle[\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}=\mathbf{r}_{2k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{g_{x1}\left(\mathbf{r}_{1k},\gamma_{1k}\right)-\alpha_{1k}\mathbf{r}_{1k}}{1-\alpha_{1k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\frac{g_{x1}\left([\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1},[\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{1}\right)-[\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{1}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}}{1-[\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{1}},

where
(a)
follows from Algorithm
2
.
The proof for
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}
follows in the same way. Next,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}=\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{1k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbf{V}^{T}\left[\mathbf{V}\mathbf{D}_{k}\left(\tau_{2k}\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{p}_{2k}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right)-\alpha_{2k}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right]/(1-\alpha_{2k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left[\mathbf{D}_{k}\left([\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}]_{1}\mathbf{S}^{T}[\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1}+\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}[\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}\right)-[\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}]_{1}[\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{1}\right]/(1-[\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}]_{1})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\left[\left[\mathbf{f}_{q}^{\mathrm{in}}\left(\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}},\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}},\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q},\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk},\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}\right)-\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}\mathbf{q}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}\right]/(1-\alpha^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk})\right]_{1},

where
(a)
follows from Algorithm
2
and the definition (15) in
[
40
]
,
(b)
follows from the definitions in (
4.1
),
and
(e)
follows from definition (
4.2
). The proof for
[\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{1}
follows in the same way using the fact that

g_{z2}\left(\mathbf{r}_{2k},\mathbf{p}_{2k},\tau_{2k},\gamma_{2k}\right)=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{D}_{k}\left[\tau_{2k}\mathbf{S}^{T}\mathbf{U}^{T}\mathbf{p}_{2k}+\gamma_{2k}\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{r}_{2k}\right].

Relative to the large system behavior of the iterates, the primary innovation of (G)VAMP can be understood in terms of the fact that its iterates can be generated by this more general recursion.
Utilizing the left and right singular vectors separately allows for the effect of
\mathbf{A}
to be broken up within the general algorithm, isolating the effects of the (rectangular diagonal) singular values matrix
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
and of the orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
and
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times M}
in various stages.

Notice that in Theorem
1
, we want to understand the concentration properties of the estimate
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}
. But this is just a function of
\mathbf{r}_{1k}
and, thus, by the above identities (
4.1
) of
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}=\mathbf{x}^{0}
. Therefore, once we have concentration for the iterates of the general recursion, we will be able to easily obtain concentration for
\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{1k}
in the proof of Theorem 1.

As this translation shows, the effect of the singular values
can be entirely subsumed within the denoiser
f_{q}
through the disturbance vectors
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{q}
. Thus, we do not require any distributional assumptions about
the singular values beyond empirical convergence to some bounded random variable (
Assumption 0
). In the case of GVAMP, we require only distributional assumptions about
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{V}
. Moreover, the matrix
\mathbf{U}
does not occur in Algorithm
4
, and it follows that the large system behavior of VAMP can be characterized using only distributional assumptions on
\mathbf{V}
.

4.1
Conditions

To prove our general concentration result, we require a number of conditions on the quantities in Algorithm
3
. Using the translations above, we show that these conditions are implied by
Assumptions 0
-
5
that we made about the GVAMP quantities in Section
3
.

Condition 0.
The initial sequences
\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{0}
are independent of each other and the matrices
\mathbf{U}
,
\mathbf{V}
and concentrate on random variables
U_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
U_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}
, respectively. This will always occur if the initializations are random samples from subgaussian prior distributions, as is guaranteed by our translation (
4.1
) and
Assumption 0
.

Furthermore, the initial
\gamma^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}
and
\gamma^{\mathrm{out}}_{p0}
converge to
\overline{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}\geq 0
and
\overline{\gamma}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p0}\geq 0
, respectively, as
N\to\infty
.
From (
4.1
), we can see that the conditions on the
\gamma^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{p0}
are guaranteed by
Assumption 0
on the corresponding constants in the GVAMP algorithm.

Condition 1.

For any
\phi\in PL(2)
, assume that the components of the sequences
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{p}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{q}
are sampled i.i.d. from subgaussian distributions.
Furthermore, we assume that the (components of the) corresponding limiting variables
W^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{p}
and
W^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{q}
are independent of
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}
(thus, independent of the limiting
P_{k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
and
Q_{k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
) and independent of each other.
Under the translation in (
4.3
), this condition is implied by
Assumption 1
for
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p}
,
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p}
, and
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{out}}_{q}
. However, we need to verify the desired concentration for
\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}=(\mathbf{s}\;\bm{0})
. Let
W^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}
be a random variable with mixture distribution
\delta\mu_{S}+(1-\delta)\mu_{0}
where
\mu_{S}
is the distribution of
S
,
\mu_{0}
is the Dirac point measure at
0
, and
\delta=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{M}{N}
. Then for any
\phi\in PL(2)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi([\mathbf{w}^{\mathrm{in}}_{q}]_{i})=\delta_{N}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\phi(\mathbf{s}_{i})+(1-\delta_{N})\phi(0)

Now using our standard concentration results (Lemmas
10
and
9
) as well as the assumed concentration of
\mathbf{s}
and
\delta_{N}
, we get that the above concentrates around
\delta\mathbb{E}\phi(S)+(1-\delta)\phi(0)=\mathbb{E}\phi(W^{\mathrm{in}}_{q})
, as needed.

Condition 2.
Same as
Assumption 2
.

Condition 3.
The denoisers
f^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{p}
and
f^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{q}
are separable, and both these functions and their derivatives are either

1.

uniformly Lipschitz at each
\left(\overline{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk},\overline{\gamma}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}\right)
and
\left(\overline{\gamma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk},\overline{\gamma}^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}\right)
, respectively, for
k\geq 1
, or

2.

uniformly bounded conditionally Lipschitz (UBCL) at the same parameters for all
k\geq 1
