0\end{matrix}\right]&\left[\begin{matrix}\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\\
0\end{matrix}\right][\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\;0]\end{matrix}\right].\end{split}

(6.115)

In the above, we have used that
\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}=\mathbb{E}\{[U_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]^{2}\}-(\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk})^{T}\left[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u(k-1)}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk}
and
\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}=\left[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u(k-1)}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk}
by (
4.41
).
Considering (
6.3
) and using Lemmas
9
and
10
, it suffices to show that
([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk})^{-1}
concentrates on
\bm{\Pi}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k-1)}
,
\|[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{vk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{-2}
concentrates on
[\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{-1}
, and
\bm{\nu}
concentrates on
[\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\;0]^{T}
. But
\mathbf{P_{k-1}.(g).(iv)}
gives us concentration for
([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk})^{-1}
, and
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(i)}
along with Lemma
A.6
gives concentration for
\|\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{vk}^{\mathrm{in}}}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{-2}
. Finally, observe that if
0\leq i\leq k-1
, we have

\left|[\bm{\nu}]_{i}-[[\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\;0]_{i}\right|\leq\left|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{ij}([\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k})-[\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{j}\right|+\left|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{i,j+(k-1)}([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k})\right|.

(6.116)

By
\mathbf{P_{k-1}.(g).(iv)}
, we get concentration for the values of
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}
. In particular, if
0\leq i,j\leq k-1
, then we have
[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{i,j}
concentrates around
[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u(k-1)}]^{-1}_{ij}
. Moreover, by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(d)}
and
\mathbf{P_{k}.(f)}
, we have that
[\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
concentrates around
[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{j}
and
[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
concentrates around
0
. Thus by Lemmas
10
and
9
, we get that the right hand term above concentrates around
0
and the left hand term concentrates around

\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u(k-1)}]^{-1}_{ij}[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{j}=\left[\left[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{u(k-1)}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk}\right]_{i}=[\bm{\beta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{i}.

The same analysis applies if
k\leq i\leq 2(k-1)
except that now for
0\leq i\leq k-1
, we have
[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{ij}
concentrates around 0 (again by
\mathbf{P_{k-1}.(g).(iv)}
), which gives us that
\bm{\nu}_{i}
concentrates around
0
, as desired. Thus, we get that the entries of
([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}
concentrate around those of
\bm{\Pi}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}
, as needed.

\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(iii)}

We will show that
\frac{1}{N}\|[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{2}
concentrates to
\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}
. To this end, observe that

\|[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{2}=[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}-[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}}[\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}=[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}-[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k},

where the second equality follows because
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}}[\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}}]^{T}T
and
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}
are both the orthogonal projection onto
\mathrm{range}\left(\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\right)
.

First observe that
\frac{1}{N}[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
concentrates around
[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{k+1,k+1}
by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(e)}
. Next observe that defining
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}=([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}
and using from the definition of
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}
in (
4.2
), we have

\begin{split}&[\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\\
&\qquad=\sum_{i,j=0}^{k}([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i})([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j})[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{i,j}+\sum_{i,j=0}^{k-1}([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i})([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j})[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{i+1+k,j+1+k}\\
&\qquad\hskip 56.9055pt+2\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i})([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j})[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{i+1+k,j}.\end{split}

Let the three terms of (
6.3
) be labeled
T_{1}-T_{3}
. For
T_{1}
, observe that
([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i})([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j})
concentrates around
0
for
0\leq i,j\leq k
by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(f)}
. Because
[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{i,j}
concentrates around
[\bm{\Pi}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk}]_{i,j}=[(\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}]_{i,j}
when
0\leq i,j\leq k
by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(ii)}
and (
4.42
), we get by Lemma
10
and
9
that
T_{1}
concentrates around
0
.

For
T_{2}
, observe that
([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{i})([\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j})
concentrates around
[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{i+1,k+1}[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{j+1,k+1}=\mathbb{E}\left[V^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}V^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[V^{\mathrm{in}}_{j}V^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\right]=[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{i}[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{j}
by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(e)}
and Lemma
10
, where the equality follows from (
4.39
) and (
4.40
).
Furthermore,

[\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{i+1+k,j+1+k}
concentrates around
[\bm{\Pi}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk}]_{i+1+k,j+1+k}=[(\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k-1)})^{-1}]_{i,j}
when
0\leq i,j\leq k
by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(ii)}
and (
4.42
).
Thus, again by Lemma
11
and Lemma
9
, we get that
T_{2}
concentrates around

\sum_{i,j=0}^{k-1}[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{i}[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]_{j}[(\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k-1)})^{-1}]_{i,j}=[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]^{T}[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k-1)}]^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}.
Using analogous reasoning,
T_{3}
also concentrates around
0
.

Combining the above with Lemma
9
and using (
4.41
), we get that
\frac{1}{N}\|[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{2}
concentrates around the desired constant:

\left[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}\right]_{k+1,k+1}-[\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]^{T}[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k-1)}]^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}=\rho^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}.

\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(iv)}
We want to show that
[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}
is invertible with high probability and that, when invertible, the entries of the inverse concentrate around

\bm{\Pi}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}=\left[\begin{matrix}[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{uk}]^{-1}&0\\
0&[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]^{-1}\end{matrix}\right].

This can be proved in much the same way as
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(ii)}
. In particular, note that
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}=[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}]
. Then applying the block inversion formula to
[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}
, we see that it is invertible if
[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}
is invertible and
\|[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{2}\neq 0
. But the probabilities of both of these events concentrate by
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(ii)}
and
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(iii)}
respectively.

Now, analogously to
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(ii)}
, to get concentration for the entries of
[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}
, it suffices to get concentration for the entries of
([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}
,
([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk})^{-1}[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
, and of
\|[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{v}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\|^{-2}
. For these, we can use
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(ii)}
and
\mathbf{P_{k}.(g).(iii)}
and apply the same arguments as above. This gives concentration for the entries of
[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{v(k+1)}
around those of
\bm{\Pi}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}
.

6.4
Showing
\mathbf{Q_{k+1}}
Holds

The arguments for this case are analogous to those in
\mathbf{P_{k}}
, so we omit them here.

7
Useful Lemmas

Many of the lemmas used in this section were originally given in
[
38
]
. We do not include the proofs of these lemmas here, and instead refer the reader to
[
38
]
for the proofs.

Lemma 8

(Products of Lipschitz Functions)
.

If
f,g:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
are Lipschitz continuous, then
h(x)=f(x)g(x):\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
is pseudo-Lipschitz of order
2
.

Lemma 9

(Concentration of Sums)
.

If random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
satisfy
P(\lvert X_{i}\rvert\geq\epsilon)\leq e^{-n\kappa_{i}\epsilon^{2}}
for
1\leq i\leq M
, then

P\Big(\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{M}X_{i}\lvert\geq\epsilon\Big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}P\left(|X_{i}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{M}\right)\leq Me^{-n(\min_{i}\kappa_{i})\epsilon^{2}/M^{2}}.

Lemma 10

(Concentration of Products)
.

For random variables
X,Y
and non-zero constants
c_{X},c_{Y}
, if

P(|X-c_{X}|\geq\epsilon)\leq Ke^{-\kappa n\epsilon^{2}},

and

P(|Y-c_{Y}|\geq\epsilon)\leq Ke^{-\kappa n\epsilon^{2}},

then the probability
P(|XY-c_{X}c_{Y}|\geq\epsilon)
is bounded by

\displaystyle P\Big(|X-c_{X}|\geq\min\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{3}},\frac{\epsilon}{3c_{Y}}\Big)\Big)+P\Big(|Y-c_{Y}|\geq\min\Big(\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon}{3}},\frac{\epsilon}{3c_{X}}\Big)\Big)\leq 2Ke^{-\frac{\kappa n\epsilon^{2}}{9\max(1,c_{X}^{2},c_{Y}^{2})}}.

Lemma 11

(Concentration of Products)
.

For random variables
X,Y,
and constant
c_{X}
, if

P(|X-c_{X}|\geq\epsilon)\leq Ke^{-r_{X}\epsilon^{2}}\text{ and }P(|Y|\geq\epsilon)\leq Ke^{-r_{Y}\epsilon^{2}},

then if
c_{X}\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle P(|XY|\geq\sqrt{\epsilon})\leq P(\lvert X-c_{X}\rvert\geq\sqrt{\epsilon})+P\Big(\lvert Y\rvert\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2\max\{1,|c_{X}|\}}\Big)\leq 2K\exp\Big\{-\frac{\epsilon^{2}\min\{r_{Y},r_{X}\}}{4\max\{1,c_{X}^{2}\}}\Big\},

and if
c_{X}=0
and
\epsilon<1
, we have

\displaystyle P(|XY|\geq\epsilon)\leq 2K\exp\Big\{-\epsilon^{2}\min\{r_{Y},r_{X}\}\Big\}.

Lemma 12

(Concentration of Square Roots)
.

Let
c\neq 0
. If
P(\lvert X_{n}^{2}-c^{2}\lvert\geq\epsilon)\leq e^{-\kappa n\epsilon^{2}},
then
P(\lvert\lvert X_{n}\rvert-\lvert c\rvert\lvert\geq\epsilon)\leq e^{-\kappa n\lvert c\rvert^{2}\epsilon^{2}}.

Lemma 13

(Concentration of Scalar Inverses)
.

Assume
c\neq 0
and
0<\epsilon<1
. If

P(\lvert X_{n}-c\lvert\geq\epsilon)\leq e^{-\kappa n\epsilon^{2}},\text{ then }P(\lvert X_{n}^{-1}-c^{-1}\lvert\geq\epsilon)\leq 2e^{-n\kappa\epsilon^{2}c^{2}\min\{c^{2},1\}/4}.

Lemma 14
.

For a random variable
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
\epsilon>0
,

P\Big(\lvert Z\rvert\geq\epsilon\Big)\leq 2e^{-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}}
.

Lemma 15

(
\chi^{2}
-concentration)
.

For
Z_{i}
,
i\in[n]
that are i.i.d.
\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, and
0\leq\epsilon\leq 1
,

P\Big(\Big\lvert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{2}-1\Big\lvert\geq\epsilon\Big)\leq 2e^{-n\epsilon^{2}/8}.

Lemma 16
.

For any scalars
a_{1},...,a_{t}
and positive integer
m
, we have
\left(\lvert a_{1}\rvert+\ldots+\lvert a_{t}\rvert\right)^{m}\leq t^{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^{t}\lvert a_{i}\rvert^{m}
.
Consequently, for any vectors
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{t}\mathbf{u}_{k}\rVert^{2}\leq t\sum_{k=1}^{t}\lVert\mathbf{u}_{k}\rVert^{2}
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be random vectors such that
(Z_{1,i},\ldots,Z_{t,i})
are i.i.d. across
i\in[n]
, with
(Z_{1,i},\ldots,Z_{t,i})
being jointly Gaussian with zero mean, unit variance and covariance matrix
K\in\mathbb{R}^{t\times t}
. Let
G\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a random vector with entries
G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
i.i.d.
\sim p_{G}
, where
p_{G}
is sub-Gaussian with variance factor
\nu
. Then for any sequence of pseudo-Lipschitz functions
\{f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{t+1}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i=1}^{N}
with common pseudo-Lipschitz constant, non-negative constants
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{t}
, and
0<\epsilon\leq 1
, we have

\displaystyle P\Big(\Big\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(\sigma_{1}Z_{1,i},\ldots,\sigma_{t}Z_{t,i},G_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}[f_{i}(Z_{1,1},\ldots,Z_{t,1},G)]\Big\lvert\geq\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leq 2\exp\Big\{\frac{-N\epsilon^{2}}{128L^{2}(t+1)^{2}(\nu+4\nu^{2}+\sum_{m=1}^{t}(\sigma_{m}^{2}+4\sigma_{m}^{4}))}\Big\},

where
L>0
is an absolute constant. (
L
can be bounded above by three times the common pseudo-Lipschitz constant of the
f_{i}
.)

Proof.

The statement of Lemma
17
is slightly more general than Lemma B.4 in
[
38
]
in that Lemma B.4 requires the functions
f_{i}
to all be equal. However, the proof of that lemma proves the above result exactly if we simply substitute the specific functions
f_{i}
for
f
throughout.
∎

8
Concentration is Preserved By Haar Matrices

Lemma 18
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{v}_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a (possibly random) vector-valued sequence such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}([\mathbf{v}_{N}]_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\phi_{i}(V)\right|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq C\exp\left(-cN\epsilon^{2}\right)

for any sequence
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset PL(2)
with a common pseudo-Lipschitz constant and some random variable
V
with finite second moment. If
\mathbf{A}_{N}
is an
N\times N
Haar-distributed orthogonal matrix independent of
\mathbf{v}_{N}
for each
N
, then

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{N}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}([\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}]_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\phi_{i}(Z)\right|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq C^{\prime}\exp\left(-c^{\prime}N\epsilon^{2}\right),

where
Z\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
for
\sigma^{2}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|^{2}
. In particular, the above statement holds if
\mathbf{v}_{N}
is a deterministic sequence.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{A}_{N}
is independent of
\mathbf{v}_{N}
and
\mathbf{A}_{N}
is Haar distributed, we have that
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}
for any orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{B}
. Thus, the distribution of
\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}
is rotationally invariant and depends only on the distribution of the norm
\|\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}\|=\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|
. Consequently, if
\mathbf{Z}_{N}\sim N(\bm{0},\Im_{N})
independent of
\mathbf{v}_{N}
, we have that

\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}\mathbf{Z}_{N}.

Then we get that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}([\mathbf{A}_{N}\mathbf{v}_{N}]_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\phi_{i}(Z)\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\phi_{i}(Z)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}\left(\sigma[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}\left(\sigma[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\phi_{i}(Z)\right|.

Now by Lemma
9
, it suffices to show concentration for these two terms separately. For the first we use the triangle inequality, the pseudo-Lipschitz property of the
\phi_{i}
, and Cauchy-Schwarz to get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}\left(\sigma[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|\phi_{i}\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)-\phi_{i}\left(\sigma[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|1+\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}+\sigma\right)[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right|\left|\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}-\sigma\right||[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq L\left|\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}-\sigma\right|\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}{\sqrt{N}}+\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}+\sigma\right)\frac{{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|^{2}}}{N}\right).

Now we know that
\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}
concentrates around
\sigma
using our assumptions on
\mathbf{v}_{N}
, Lemma
15
, and Lemma
. Then using this fact along with Lemmas
12
,
10
, and
15
, we get that
\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}{\sqrt{N}}+\left(\frac{\|\mathbf{v}_{N}\|}{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|}+\sigma\right)\frac{{\|\mathbf{Z}_{N}\|^{2}}}{N}\right)
concentrates around a finite value (specifically
1+2\sigma
). Combining these with Lemma
10
, we get that the the above concentrates with the desired rates (since each of our intermediate lemmas preserve this
N\epsilon^{2}
rate). Finally concentration for

\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}\left(\sigma[\mathbf{Z}_{N}]_{i}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\phi_{i}(Z)\right|

follows directly from Lemma
17
(again with the desired rate) since we assume that the
\phi_{i}
share a common pseudo-Lipschitz constant. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

9
Extension of Concentration Analysis to Matrix General Recursion

In this appendix, we outline the adjustments that need to made to the main concentration arguments in order to get the analogous concentration result for the general recursion in Algorithm
3
with
d>1
(i.e. with matrix rather than vector iterates). The main differences lie in accounting for the distributions of the asymptotic variables
P_{k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
and
Q_{k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
, which are now zero-mean Gaussian vectors rather than simply Gaussian random variables. As we will see, this adjustment follows directly from a straightforward extension of Lemma
3
, which in turn relies on the following characterization of orthogonally invariant random matrices.

Lemma 19
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
for
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
a Haar distributed orthogonal matrix and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
an independent random matrix which has full rank with probability
1
. Furthermore, let
\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be the Gram matrix of
\mathbf{x}
, and let
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be a random matrix with independent standard Gaussian entries which is independent of
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{x}
. Then we have that

\mathbf{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}^{-1/2}_{\mathbf{z}}\mathbf{G}^{1/2}_{\mathbf{y}}

(9.1)

Proof.

(
informal
)

The following proof is only informal because its claims rest on statements about probabilities of measure zero events. The difficulty of making these informal arguments more formal arises from the fact that the relevant random quantities take values in the space of
n\times d
matrices with orthonormal columns. As this space is non-Euclidean, probability densities on this space do not satisfy the usual change of variables formula (since the normal definition of the Jacobian does not make sense). I believe this may be fixable using a change of variables formula from geometric measure theory that employs a suitable generalization of the Jacobian.

Let
\Phi(\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{c}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{c}}^{-1/2}
for any matrix
\mathbf{c}
. Then note that following facts:

1.

\Phi(\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
has orthonormal columns.

2.

\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{c}}=\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{c}}
for any orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{V}
.

3.

\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{x}
for any fixed orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{V}
since
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{U}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{U}
are independent.

Now let
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be the map which simply drops the last
n-d
columns of its input. Then let
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be any matrix with orthonormal columns and let
\mathbf{V}
be an orthogonal matrix such that
\pi(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
. Then by the above facts, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{x}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{x}}^{-1/2}=\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{G}^{-1/2}_{\mathbf{x}}\pi(\mathbf{I})\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{x})=\rho(\mathbf{I})\right).

Since this is true for any
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
is uniform over
{d\times n}
matrices with orthonormal columns.

Now since
\mathbf{z}
has independent standard Gaussian entries, we have that
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{z}
, and thus

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{z}}^{-1/2}=\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{V}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{z}}^{-1/2}=\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{z}}^{-1/2}=\rho(\mathbf{I})\right).

This implies that
\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}^{-1/2}_{\mathbf{z}}=\Phi(\mathbf{x})
.

Finally, we note that the entirety of the above argument still goes through with
\mathbf{y}
fixed, which implies that
\Phi(\mathbf{x})
is uniformly distributed conditional of any particular of
\mathbf{y}
and is thus independent of
\mathbf{y}
. Therefore,

\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{x}}^{-1/2}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{x}}^{1/2}=\Phi(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{y}}^{1/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{z}\mathbf{G}^{-1/2}_{\mathbf{z}}\mathbf{G}^{1/2}_{\mathbf{y}},

as claimed.
∎

Using this, we obtain a matrix version of Lemma
3
. To state this lemma, we first note the necessary adjustments to our notation. The matrices
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{qk},

\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}
,
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{uk}
, and
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{vk}
are defined in exactly the same way as before, where now we understand the iterates (e.g.
\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{j}
) as
(N/M)\times d
matrices, so that, e.g.,
\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}
is now a
N\times d(2k+1)
matrix. Likewise, the sigma algebras
\mathcal{P}_{j}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
retain their definitions in terms of these matrix iterates. With
\mathbf{C}
equal to any of these matrices, the definitions of the corresponding matrices
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}}^{\perp}
are unchanged. The Gaussian quantities
\mathbf{Z}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}
,
\breve{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}
, and
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}
have i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries before, but are expanded to have
d
columns so that, e.g.
\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}\in\mathbb{R}^{(N/M)\times d}
. Finally, the scalar/vector parameters
\rho_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
and
\beta_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
are replaced by matrix parameters
\mathbf{P}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbf{B}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{kd\times d}
respectively. We also introduce an additional indexing notation. For each
1\leq i\leq k
letting

[\mathbf{B}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}]_{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

be the
d\times d
submatrix with top-left entry
[\mathbf{B}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}]_{d(i-1)+1,1}
. In other words, if we view
\mathbf{B}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
as a
k
-dimensional vector with
d\times d
matrix entries, then this is just accessing the index
i
component of this vector.

With this notation, we can now state our generalized lemma. To save space, we state this only for the
\mathbf{p}
iterates, but the extension to the
\mathbf{q}
iterates is completely symmetric.

Lemma 20
.

If
[\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}
has full rank for
0\leq k\leq K
, then we have for all such
k
that

\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0}\lvert_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{O}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}]^{1/2}+\bm{\Delta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}\lvert_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\,\mathbf{p}^{\mathrm{in}}_{\ell}\,[\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{(\ell+1)}+\mathbf{O}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{1/2}+\bm{\Delta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk},

(9.2)

where
\mathbf{O}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}
is defined in (
4.8
), and

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}

\displaystyle\text{ given in {Condition 0}},

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{vk}\left(([\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}]^{T}\mathbf{C}^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk})^{-1}[\mathbf{C}_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}}]^{T}\mathbf{u}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}-\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\\
\bm{0}_{dk\times d}\end{matrix}\right]\right)+\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{vk}^{\mathrm{in}}}\mathbf{Z}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\left[\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{Z}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}}^{-1/2}\mathbf{G}_{[\mathbf{B}^{\perp}_{\mathbf{C}_{qk}^{\mathrm{in}}}]^{T}\mathbf{u}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}}^{1/2}-[\mathbf{P}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}]^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\mathbf{B}_{\mathbf{C}_{vk}^{\mathrm{in}}}\,\breve{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}[\mathbf{P}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}}]^{1/2}.

Furthermore, we have that
\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\mathbf{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{P}_{k}
for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

The proof of this statement is essentially identical to that of Lemma
3
, merely replacing applications of Lemma
1
with Lemma
19
and substituting our matrix parameters
\mathbf{P}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbf{B}_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}\in\mathbb{R}^{kd\times d}
for
\rho_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
and
\beta_{(p/q)k}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
.
∎

Next we can state an analog of Lemma
4
. This Lemma depends on a matrix version of the Gaussian equivalent recursion in Algorithm
5
. We omit the explicit statement here, as it is defined exactly to make statement
(2)
in the following lemma true (and hence is entirely characterized by the statement of this lemma).

Lemma 21
.

First define

\begin{split}\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{0}=\widetilde{\mathbf{O}}_{p0}^{\mathrm{in}}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{in}}_{p0}]^{1/2},&\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}=\sum_{r=0}^{k-1}\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{r}^{\mathrm{in}}[\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{(r+1)}+\,\widetilde{\mathbf{O}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{1/2},\\
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{0}=\widetilde{\mathbf{O}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p0}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p0}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{out}}_{p0}]^{1/2},&\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}=\sum_{r=0}^{k-1}\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{r}^{\mathrm{out}}[\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{(r+1)}+\widetilde{\mathbf{O}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]^{1/2}.\end{split}

(9.3)

Similarly define
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
and
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{k}
. With these definitions, we have

1.

\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}=\sum_{r=0}^{k}\widetilde{\mathbf{O}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pr}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pr}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]^{1/2}\,[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{r},\qquad\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}=\sum_{r=0}^{k}\widetilde{\mathbf{O}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pr}\,\overline{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pr}\,[\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]^{1/2}\,[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{r}

(9.4)

where for
k\geq 1
and
0\leq r\leq k-1
,
[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are defined recursively as

[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{r}=\sum_{i=r}^{k-1}[\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{(i+1)}[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pi}]_{r},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{r}=\sum_{i=r}^{k-1}[\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{(i+1)}[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pi}]_{r},

(9.5)

with
[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{k}=[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{k}=\mathsf{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
for all
k\geq 0
.

2.

Let
\underline{\accentset{*}{\mathbf{r}}}_{k}=\left(\accentset{*}{\mathbf{r}}_{0},\ldots,\accentset{*}{\mathbf{r}}_{k}\right)
where
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{r}}_{j}=(\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j},\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{j},\accentset{*}{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{j},\accentset{*}{\mathbf{q}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{j})
are defined as above for the ideal variables
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}
and
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{q}}
. Then we have that

\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{r}}}_{k}-\underline{\accentset{*}{\mathbf{r}}}_{k}=\left(\mathbf{d}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{d}_{k}\right),

(9.6)

where
\underline{\widetilde{\mathbf{r}}}_{k}
is defined in (
4.22
) with

\mathbf{d}_{k}=\left(\sum_{r=0}^{k}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pr}\,[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{pk}]_{r},\,\sum_{r=0}^{k}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pr}\,[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{pk}]_{r},\,\sum_{r=0}^{k}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{qr}\,[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{in}}_{qk}]_{r},\,\sum_{r=0}^{k}\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{qr}\,[\mathbf{c}^{\mathrm{out}}_{qk}]_{r}\right).

(9.7)

Furthermore, for all
k\geq 0
, we have that

\widetilde{\underline{\mathbf{r}}}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\underline{\mathbf{r}}_{k}.

(9.8)

3.

For all
i\geq 1
, we have
\left([\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{i},\ldots,[\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{\mathrm{in}}]_{i},[\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{i},\ldots,[\accentset{*}{\mathbf{p}}_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}]_{i}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left(P^{\mathrm{in}}_{0},\ldots,P^{\mathrm{in}}_{k},P_{0}^{\mathrm{out}},\ldots,P_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}\right)
where
(P_{0}^{\mathrm{in}},\ldots,P_{k}^{\mathrm{in}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d(k+1)}
and
(P_{0}^{\mathrm{out}},\ldots,P_{k}^{\mathrm{out}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d(k+1)}
are independent, zero-mean, jointly Gaussian vectors (and hence the right hand side is itself jointly Gaussian).

Analogous statements for (1.)-(3.) hold for the
\accentset{*}{\mathbf{q}}
variables.

Proof.

The proof of this statement is again essentially identical to that of Lemma
4
once all vector quantities are replaced by their matrix analogs.
∎

Finally, we can define limiting quantities for the matrix form of the general recursion. Specifically, if we set

\displaystyle\overline{U}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\left([U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{0}]^{T},\ldots,[U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}]^{T}\right)^{T}

\displaystyle=\left(\left[U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{0}\right]_{1},\ldots,\left[U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{0}\right]_{d},\ldots,\left[U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}\right]_{1},\ldots,\left[U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}\right]_{d}\right)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(k+1)},

then we can define

\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\overline{U}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}[\overline{U}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}]^{T}\right\}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(k+1)\times d(k+1)},\quad\mathbf{b}_{uk}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\overline{U}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k-1}[U^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{k}]^{T}\right\}\in\mathbb{R}^{kd\times d}.

(9.9)

In terms of these, we can then define

\mathbf{B}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}=\left[\bm{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{p(k-1)}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{b}_{uk},\quad\mathbf{P}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}_{pk}=\left[\bm{\Sigma}_{pk}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}\right]_{(k,k)}-[\mathbf{b}_{uk}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}]^{T}\left[\bm{\Sigma}_{p(k-1)}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}\right]^{-1}\mathbf{b}_{uk}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}},

(9.10)

where the
(i,j)
indexing notation is used to extract the
d\times d
submatrix with top left index
((i-1)d+1,(j-1)d+1)
.

With these results, it is easy to see how the proof of Lemma
6
extend to the matrix case. For parts
(g)
and
(a)
, which are concerned with the broad components of the
\bm{\Delta}
terms, the only differences arise from the presence of matrix operations replacing scalar operations (in particular due to the introduction of the Gram matrices). Concentration for matrix multiplications can be established as we have done for other matrix products in the proof of Lemma
6
(specifically using our concentration results for scalar products and sums). Concentration for matrix square roots is more complicated, but for
d\leq 4
, explicit formulas in terms of radicals are possible, to which are existing suite of concentration tools can be applied. In each case, the matrix operations which replace scalar operations are all of dimension
d
, which is fixed and independent of
N
and
k
. Hence, whatever penalty is incurred in the concentration rate we expect to be proportional to
d^{2}
. In particular, we can view this as being absorbed into the universal constants
c
and
C
.

Part
(b)
of the proof is almost entirely analogous as the same arguments now simply need to be applied to pseudo-Lipschitz functions with
2kd
rather than
2k
inputs. The remaining parts of the proof are essentially just applications of
(b)
to establish concentration for various inner products, which can also be established in exactly the same way.
